#5126. A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

Bitcoin Core developers have disclosed three security vulnerabilities in the software that runs most of the Bitcoin network, including one high-severity bug that could disrupt mining, all of which have since been fixed.

Bitcoin Core, the reference software operating the majority of the network’s nodes, classifies vulnerabilities into four tiers, from “low” to “critical,” and these fell into the middle of that range.

The most serious, tracked as CVE-2025-62861, concerns block template creation, the process miners rely on to assemble new blocks.

According to the advisory, a chain reorganization could trigger an erroneous eviction of certain transactions, corrupting a node’s mempool, its waiting room for unconfirmed transactions, so that an affected node could no longer build block templates until it was restarted. For a miner, that means a halt to the very work that earns block rewards.

A second flaw, CVE-2025-62859, could let a peer stall a node’s block processing by announcing a compact block with a manipulated witness, a form of transaction malleation.

None of the three rises to the “critical” level Bitcoin Core reserves for bugs that threaten coin theft at the protocol level, unauthorized coin creation or permanent network-wide chain splits. The developers did not report any evidence the flaws were exploited.

The disclosures come amid a broader reckoning over how quickly Bitcoin’s defenders can keep pace with a shifting threat landscape.

Bitcoin Core recently rewrote its security policy to account for AI, adding a category for “easily discoverable” vulnerabilities that can be surfaced through a simple AI prompt, which it now patches and discloses immediately rather than holding under a long embargo.

That shift reflects mounting concern that large language models have lowered the bar for finding flaws, a worry underscored by the volunteer “Bitcoin Red Team,” which has been racing to uncover AI-discoverable bugs across the ecosystem before attackers can exploit them.

The fixes themselves shipped earlier, with the advisories following under Bitcoin Core’s disclosure timeline. Node operators are generally urged to run the latest software to stay protected.

Tron, Tether, and Chainalysis have formed the "Digital Crime Task Force" to combat illicit crypto activities amid a surge in AI-enabled crime.

In a statement shared with The Block, the trio said the new initiative is designed to identify and disrupt illicit activities involving Tether's USDT on the Tron (TRON) blockchain.

The new task force comes as AI-powered scams and cyberattacks pose growing threats to the crypto industry.

Chainalysis flagged in the statement that AI-enabled scams were 4.5 times more profitable than conventional schemes last year, with impersonation scams surging more than 1,400% year over year.

"Criminals are using AI to move faster than ever, and waiting for a victim to report a crime is no longer good enough," said Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino. "Tether has already frozen more than $7 billion linked to illicit activity, working with more than 360 law enforcement agencies across 73 countries."

The task force noted that it plans to focus on a wide range of threats, including investment and romance scams, hacks, terrorist financing, and North Korean cyberattacks.

Blockchain infrastructure provider Chainstack also disclosed that it experienced an AI-powered security breach late last month that exposed customer names, emails, and password hashes.

#5121. What Are Solana Privacy Rings? Inside Helius’ Privacy Protocol for Solana

Every transaction on Solana is public by default. Anyone can look up a wallet address and see its balance, what it has sent and received, and when. That transparency is useful for verifying the network, but a problem for anyone who doesn't want their finances on display: a business paying salaries, a trader building a position, or an individual who simply doesn't want strangers seeing their savings.

Rings are programmable, onchain environments for encrypting money, markets, and finance - without ever leaving Solana

Now live on mainnet. pic.twitter.com/ezCIUCjcbI

Rings support SOL and other tokens on Solana, including SPL and Token-2022 tokens. Users keep custody of their funds throughout, since every transaction needs the owner's signature.

Crucially, the private balances live on the same network as everything else. Private transactions execute directly on Solana and encrypted data is stored on Solana's ledger, with no separate layer-2 network or sequencer in between. That means private balances sit alongside the same stablecoins, tokenized assets and DeFi liquidity as public ones.

Each private transfer comes with a zero-knowledge proof, a piece of cryptography that lets the network confirm the transfer is valid, for example that the sender actually had the funds, without revealing the numbers involved. Rings use a type of zero-knowledge proof called a Groth16 zk-SNARK, which Helius says can be generated in tens of milliseconds, either by a service provider or on the user's own device. Each private transfer settles in a single Solana transaction.

Users read their own encrypted balances with a viewing key. They can decrypt their balance locally, or let a service provider decrypt it and serve it to them, and they can share a key for a single transaction with an auditor or counterparty without exposing the rest of their history.

Moving funds into a Ring from an ordinary public wallet, or back out again, is visible onchain, including the asset and amount. But users who on-ramp from fiat currency straight into a private balance, through a supported provider, keep the asset and amount hidden from the start, according to Helius.

Because Custom Rings interoperate with the default Ring, regulated firms can keep their activity confidential from the market while still sharing the same liquidity as everyone else.

Private transfers are just the start. Solana programs can hold encrypted balances in private escrows and release them when set conditions are met, opening the way for:

At launch, Decrypt, mining and staking app ORE and cross-chain service Aurora Intents were using the default Ring, while Backpack, DFlow, Jupiter, Squads and Titan were among the teams working on integrations.

Helius says the core programs and zero-knowledge circuits have been audited by security firms Certora, OtterSec and ZK Security. Engineers from the Solana Foundation, Blueshift, Zcash and other teams took part in the "trusted setup," the one-off ceremony that generates the parameters the proofs rely on.

The code is open source, published on GitHub under the Apache 2.0 license, so anyone can inspect how it works.

Formal verification, a mathematical check that the circuits behave exactly as intended, is still underway and expected to finish by the end of 2026. Solana's own documentation currently describes Rings as being in beta.

#5119. BitGo expands institutional stablecoin minting and redemption platform with USDC

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Clients of BitGo's federally regulated trust bank can use their Go Account to place orders without opening a separate account with Circle.

BitGo BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) has added USDC (USDC) minting and redemption to its Go Accounts, the digital asset infrastructure firm said Thursday.

The service is available to eligible customers of BitGo Bank & Trust, its digital asset trust bank subsidiary regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Clients can initiate and monitor their USDC orders on the BitGo platform or through its API, according to a statement shared with The Block. BitGo will route them through its Circle Mint account, charging flat fees that clients see before they confirm the transaction.

BitGo initially offered minting and redemption for World Liberty Financial's USD1 and SoFi Bank's SoFiUSD when it first launched its Mint service in April, enabling institutions to access supported assets without managing a separate relationship with each issuer.

"The more assets an institution uses, the more work it takes to manage separate accounts and issuer relationships," BitGo CEO and co-founder Mike Belshe said in the statement. "We're building on the infrastructure our clients already use so they can access more assets through one BitGo account."

The service supports USDC on Ethereum, Solana, Base, Arbitrum, Polygon, Avalanche, HyperEVM, Monad, and Aptos, though BitGo said availability depends on jurisdiction and other account requirements.

The onchain components of eligible orders can be processed 24 hours a day, seven days a week, subject to network availability and account controls, according to the firm. However, clients funding or withdrawing fiat will still have to work within the applicable banking processing windows and settlement times.

The news comes after last week's launch of BitGo Global Markets, led by former NYDIG executive Pete Janney, to offer institutional trading and financial services including crypto-backed financing, options, and swaps. That launch followed its acquisition of NYDIG's institutional trading business on Aug. 27. About 30 employees joined BitGo following the acquisition.

#5118. I Work Out for a Living—Here Are the Best Headphones for Everything From Runs to Gym Sessions

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Because every sweat session needs an epic soundtrack.

WHETHER YOU PREFER to listen to music, a podcast, or tune into sports while getting your sweat on, you'll need a solid pair of workout headphones to make it happen. Beyond a high-quality pre-workout, nothing elevates your training quite like the perfect pair of headphones. These devices not only drown out grunting gym-goers but also help you stay focused, find your rhythm, and push through those last few reps when your body is telling you to quit.

The key to a good pair of workout headphones is finding ones that fit your ears snugly and won't budge while you run or do kettlebell swings. Workout headphones need water resistance to handle sweat and 5-mile runs in the rain. We can recommend eleven pairs of workout headphones for a range of training environments.

Like our favorite running shoes, modern workout headphones are built for performance, combining durability with athletic-minded features. Sweat and water resistance are non-negotiable, while a secure, comfortable fit prevents them from falling out during burpees, sprints, or heavy lifts. Meanwhile, battery life has improved dramatically in recent years. Many models offer enough juice to power through a week’s worth of workouts on a single charge. Need to focus? Noise cancellation, intuitive controls, and quick-pairing technology keep you in the zone rather than fumbling with your tech.

Whether you’re just starting your fitness journey or a seasoned athlete, the best workout headphones can transform your training sessions. The Men’s Health fitness and tech editors tested a few dozen options to identify the eleven best models available. From true wireless earbuds to over-ear models to bone conduction headphones, these top-performing headphones deliver impressive durability, high-quality sound, and workout-specific features to help you push through the pain.

The AirPods Pro have improved significantly over the past few updates, and the latest version, the AirPods Pro 3, is what we believe are the best do-it-all workout headphones. These earbuds deliver excellent comfort, premium features, and high-quality sound. Apple has systematically addressed every pain point we experienced with earlier models, transforming these wireless earbuds from casual earbuds into essential fitness companions.

The fit is a major reason the AirPods Pro work so well during workouts, even surpassing the latest Apple release, the Apple AirPods 5. After dozens of reps, we found they don’t budge. The multiple silicone ear tips let our testing team find the perfect seal during burpees and box jumps. Despite wearing them for multiple training sessions (and in the office), we experienced no ear fatigue thanks to their lightweight design.

The improved ergonomics also contribute to better passive noise isolation, working with active noise cancellation to block out excessively loud gym music. For athletes who rely on music for motivation during workouts, the secure, comfortable fit eliminates the constant annoyance of loose earbuds that need readjusting. Their IP57 rating provides additional peace of mind, ensuring they can handle the sweat that comes with serious workouts.

MH Senior Fitness Editor Brett Williams, NASM, found the most exciting new feature of the AirPods Pro 3s to be their ability to monitor your heart rate through your ear canals, a capability more commonly associated with devices that wrap around your wrist or chest. They aren't the first headphones to do so, but having this added to Apple's already impressive offerings only strengthens their case.

Battery life exceeded our expectations, with up to eight hours of continuous playback and a charging case that extends total listening time to 24 hours. The case supports wireless charging, making it easy to recharge on a charging pad. Meanwhile, the AirPods Pro 3 support several advanced features like adaptive audio, conversation awareness, and transparency mode, the latter of which lets outside sound in, so you can be aware of what’s going on around you during a run.

The touch controls are intuitive during workouts—you can play/pause, skip tracks, answer a call, and adjust the volume by pressing the stem. For iPhone owners, the AirPods are a cinch to set up. Simply open the case next to your phone, tap Connect, and you’re ready to go. This combination of comfort, performance, and ease of use makes the AirPods Pro 3 the best overall workout headphones, setting a high bar for others to match.

Read More: Our Review of the Apple AirPods Pro 3s

While the Beats Studio Pro aren’t specifically designed for working out, they’re by no means out of place in a gym. The headphones maintain the sleek, recognizable Beats aesthetic and bass-heavy sound the brand is known for, while offering premium features that make them great for training and office use.

The over-ear design provides top-notch sound isolation through plush memory foam ear cushions, while the adjustable headband distributes weight evenly across your head. They balance comfort with stability—the headband applies just enough pressure to keep the headphones secure without becoming uncomfortable during extended wear. During testing, the Studio Pro remained firmly in place through moderate workouts like weightlifting and walks, though they’re not ideal for high-impact exercises.

Battery life is fantastic, lasting up to 40 hours with ANC off (24 hours with ANC on). When the battery gets low, you can get up to 4 hours of playback with just 10 minutes of USB-C charging. The Studio Pro offers excellent cross-OS compatibility, including quick pairing and Find My capabilities. However, features like spatial audio and dynamic head tracking are exclusive to iPhone owners.

That said, the Studio Pro omits some features that are standard on other premium models, like ear detection, so they don’t automatically pause the music when you remove them from your head. Still, the headphones provide impressive performance wrapped in the brand’s iconic style.

The Powerbeats Pro 2 by Beats are a fresh addition to the workout headphone market and have quickly become our top choice among models with ear hooks. Nearly six years after the original, Beats has introduced major upgrades like active noise cancellation, transparency mode, and heart rate monitoring—a feature typically found in smartwatches. Setting up these headphones was a breeze, with the same seamless iPhone connectivity you’d expect from AirPods.

We prefer the fit and look of the Powerbeats Pro 2 compared to the first generation. The previous model’s ear hooks felt awkward, but the new design is much more comfortable, with five different ear tip sizes for a secure fit. During testing, these headphones felt glued to our ears—even during a windy run—and didn’t flinch after getting drenched in sweat thanks to the IPX4 rating. The softer plastic avoids the slimy feel after intense workouts, allowing them to transition quickly from the gym to the office.

In addition to a comfortable fit, the sound quality has improved significantly, with balanced bass that enhances the listening experience without overpowering it. We appreciated the intuitive controls, with a button on each earbud for volume control—clicking the right to increase and the left to decrease. Pausing and skipping tracks was also easy by tapping the sides of each earbud. Making calls was straightforward, with excellent sound quality, and the battery percentage displayed more precisely on our phone than other headphones, which often rounded to the nearest 10 percent.

Each earbud features a built-in sensor—the same tech found in the Apple Watch—for heart rate monitoring. It’s a nice feature, providing valuable insights into your performance during workouts. However, if you already own an Apple Watch, this feature might feel redundant.

While there’s a lot to like about the Powerbeats Pro 2, the side buttons can be overly sensitive. During runs, we occasionally found ourselves accidentally pausing or skipping tracks while adjusting the fit or changing the volume. If you own an iPhone, you’ll get the most value from these earbuds, benefiting from features like dynamic head tracking, automatic device switching, and Find My integration. However, Android users can still enjoy the earbuds with one-touch pairing, customizable controls, and the Locate My Beats feature available through the Beats app.

The Shokz OpenRun Pro 2 holds the top spot among the best bone conduction headphones and was featured in the 2025 Men’s Health Tech Awards, so it’s no surprise they’re a favorite here.

The classic wraparound design and open-ear style are incredibly comfortable, particularly during longer runs. The lightweight titanium frame rests against your temples rather than in your ear canals, eliminating the discomfort and ear fatigue you might experience with traditional earbuds. The design feels funky at first, but once you get used to it, you’ll appreciate not having anything in your ears.

After testing the OpenRun Pro 2 on both long and short runs, we found the design goes beyond comfort. Perhaps the most significant advantage is the enhanced safety it provides. Unlike traditional earbuds that can block external sounds, these headphones leave your ear canals completely unobstructed, so you stay fully aware of your surroundings. This is crucial for runners who need to hear approaching vehicles and other hazards.

Despite the open-ear design, the OpenRun Pro 2 produces high-quality sound. Shokz’s DualPitch tech helps eliminate unwanted vibrations, so audio comes through clearly—something bone conduction headphones struggle with. Even in noisy environments like busy streets or gyms, we didn’t have to max out the volume to hear our favorite tunes. In addition to its lightweight design, the headphones offer up to 12 hours of battery life and an IP55 rating, so they’ll hold up to your toughest workouts.

The Bose Ultra Open earbuds depart from traditional in-ear designs, with a clip-on style that rests against your ear rather than inside it. This unconventional approach keeps you aware of your surroundings while still delivering premium features and sound quality. For runners or cyclists, they’re a compelling alternative to conventional earbuds and bone conduction headphones.

Setup is straightforward, with quick pairing with both Android and iOS devices, and a Bose Music app that lets you customize EQ settings and update the firmware. While the design takes some getting used to, particularly getting them just right on your ear, the lightweight design is comfortable, with little to no ear fatigue. We worried the clip-on style would slip during quick head movements, but they stayed secure and never jostled loose.

They don’t match the experience of noise-canceling earbuds, but the audio performance strikes an impressive balance for anyone who needs to remain aware of their surroundings. At $30, the JLab Go Sport+ is the perfect entry-level option for runners. The flexible earhooks keep the buds locked in place, the sound is decent enough to keep you moving, and an IP55 rating means sweat isn’t a concern. Be Aware mode lets ambient sound in when you need it, and the JLab app offers EQ presets if you want to tweak the sound.

Google’s second-generation earbuds build on their predecessor with improved ANC, a more comfortable design, and enhanced sound quality, making them an easy choice for Google users.

The silicone ear tips create a secure seal, while a small “stabilizer” (basically a tiny fin) on each earbud helps them stay in place when you’re moving around. With an IPX4 rating, the Buds Pro 2 handle sweat without issue, so you can use them on a rainy-day run. The matte finish on both the buds and the case resists fingerprints and maintains a premium feel even after extended use.

Sound quality has been upgraded thanks to new 11mm dynamic drivers. Google’s spatial audio delivers an immersive listening experience for compatible content, while the Tensor-powered adaptive EQ automatically adjusts to your environment. The improved noise cancellation is particularly impressive, effectively blocking noise. Touch controls on each earbud are responsive without being overly sensitive, and the conversation detection feature automatically pauses music when you start speaking.

Battery life is around eight hours with ANC enabled, with the case providing an additional 30 hours. A quick five-minute charge delivers about one-and-a-half hours of listening time in a pinch. Overall, the Buds Pro 2 are the best wireless earbuds for Pixel phone owners, offering excellent comfort, sound quality, and durability. Plus, you get powerful features like Live Translate and Gemini support, making them more than just workout headphones.

Android users have no shortage of options, including those on this list. But Samsung’s extremely lightweight Galaxy Buds 3 Pro stand out, offering excellent features and performance for everyday use and workouts. Their AirPods-like stem design is more comfortable and secure than the Buds 2 Pro, with silicone ear tips (available in 3 sizes) that create an effective seal without causing discomfort during extended wear. They’ll stay firmly in place through running, HIIT workouts, and strength training sessions.

The IP57 rating provides excellent protection against both dust and water, making these earbuds capable of surviving temporary submersion up to 1 meter. If you like to rough it while working out, these earbuds are durable enough. The Galaxy Buds 3 Pro earbuds also deliver high-quality sound, with an adaptive mode that blends ANC based on your surroundings. These features work seamlessly, so you can hear important sound cues like sirens more easily.

Powerful voice controls elevate the hands-free experience, letting you manage playback, adjust volume, and toggle between listening modes without a specific wake phrase—simply say “volume up,” and the earbuds will increase the volume. While these earbuds get just about everything right, they lack true multipoint connectivity, meaning they can’t maintain simultaneous active connections to multiple devices. However, Samsung users benefit from Auto Switch technology, which automatically transitions the earbuds between your Galaxy phone, tablet, and laptop whenever you start media on a different device.

For people whose ears tend to reject standard earbuds, the Powerbeats Fit offer a secure alternative with its flexible wingtips and snug, workout-friendly design. Fit can vary by ear shape, and one of our testers initially experienced soreness from the wingtips—an issue that eased with shorter wear times and a switch to smaller ear tips. Once adjusted, the seal improved both comfort and sound quality. The wingtips effectively prevent slippage during intense workouts, though you need to place them carefully to keep them secure.

While other headphones with ear hooks may feel even more secure, the Powerbeats Fit offers a compelling blend of stability, sound, and battery life. At $200, they’re a worthwhile pick for anyone who needs earbuds that reliably stay put during tough workouts.

The 6.8mm dynamic drivers deliver a low-end that’s full and punchy without overpowering the mids, which is exactly what you want when a heavy playlist is the only thing getting you through a final set. JBL’s Twistlock wing design twists into your ear, keeping them in place during jumping exercises.

The IP68 rating means these can handle full submersion, not just sweat. ANC and Smart Ambient mode let you toggle between blocking out the gym and staying aware of traffic on a run. Battery life tops out at 12 hours per charge, with the case extending that to 48 hours total. That’s enough to last a long weekend without needing to find a wall outlet.

If you go hard during your workouts, you'll sweat—probably a lot. You need headphones that can keep up. Look for headphones or earbuds that are at least water-resistant or, even better, waterproof. Really, earbuds usually offer better sweat resistance than over-ear headphones. Over-ear headphones with sweat-resistant construction are a lot harder to find, although brands like Sony and Beats still hold up well to sweat. If most of your workouts are moderate, sweat resistance may not matter, and any pair of the workout headphones we've noted below will work for you.

Your favorite gym clothes aren't the only things drenched in sweat after a workout. So durability for workout headphones starts with water and sweat protection, measured by the standardized Ingress Protection (IP) rating system. The rating has two numbers: the first indicates dust protection (6 being the highest), and the second indicates water resistance (9 being the highest).

For serious athletes (and seriously sweaty workouts), we recommend headphones that offer water protection to survive intense training sessions. If you primarily work out indoors, you can get by without dust protection, but consider proper dust resistance if you’re trail running or doing HIIT on the beach. Beyond dust and water resistance, the best workout headphones boast impact-resistant materials, such as durable plastic, high-grade silicone or rubber, and reinforced hinges and headbands.

Comfort and fit are essential for peak performance. While these factors can be highly personal, certain features separate the headphones you’ll love wearing from those that’ll end up at the bottom of your gym bag.

Several types of workout headphones suit different preferences. In-ear models are the most popular for workouts thanks to their compact size, and they come in several variations. The first type features interchangeable silicone tips in small, medium, and large sizes, creating a customized seal in your ear canal. Another design incorporates flexible ear hooks that loop over your ears for added stability.

If you don’t like wearing earbuds, on-ear headphones provide cushioned pads that rest against your ears, while over-ear models create a complete seal around each ear. Bone conduction headphones offer a unique solution by conducting sound through your cheekbones while keeping your ear canals open. These are a good option for anyone who needs to maintain awareness of their surroundings, such as when running at night or in a busy city.

When you’re crushing a grueling rowing routine or leg day, the last thing you want is your headphones dying mid-workout. Look for wireless earbuds that provide at least six to eight hours of continuous playback, or on-ear/over-ear headphones that can go 20+ hours on a single charge. That should last through a full week of workouts.

The good news is most earbuds come with charging cases that can extend total battery life by several hours; it’s like having a portable battery with you at all times. Additionally, features like wireless charging and fast charging make it even more convenient to keep your headphones powered up, whether you’re dropping them on a charging pad before bed or need a quick boost before heading out the door.

Obtuse controls can throw off your entire rhythm, so look for earbuds and headphones with simple buttons or touch controls. The best workout headphones feature intuitive controls that let you skip tracks, adjust the volume, and answer phone calls—all without touching your phone. Some headphones even offer customizable controls through companion apps, allowing you to tailor the experience to your needs.

Connectivity should be equally seamless. That means pairing quickly with your smartphone without jumping through menus and Bluetooth settings. For instance, the AirPods Pro 3 and Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 are easy to pair with an iPhone—simply open the earbuds’ case near your device and tap to connect. Once paired, your AirPods can automatically connect to all your Apple devices through iCloud. Android users can take advantage of Fast Pair technology, which offers a similar one-tap connection experience with compatible headphones.

Additionally, many models now support multipoint pairing, allowing you to seamlessly switch between your phone and computer without manually disconnecting and reconnecting your headphones each time. Voice assistant integration adds another layer of hands-free convenience, letting you control your music, set a timer, or make a phone call using just your voice.

For those of us who work in busy offices, active noise cancellation (ANC) can be a game-changer for blocking out distractions. In gyms, this technology is just as useful, drowning out clanking weights, loud conversations, and blaring music.

However, the best workout headphones don’t just block out noise—they also let noise in. For outdoor workouts (or simply walking down the street), a transparency mode is an essential safety feature that uses the headphones’ microphones to pipe in ambient noise, such as approaching vehicles and emergency sirens. The most effective models let you switch between ANC and transparency modes with a simple tap or voice command.

Beyond noise cancellation, adaptive audio can automatically adjust the sound based on your environment, so you don’t have to constantly fiddle with volume controls.

For the past six years, Men's Health's fitness and tech editors have been testing dozens of the top workout headphones for men. Experts, including Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S., Senior Fitness Editor Brett Williams, NASM, Fitness and Commerce Editor Charles Thorp, NASM, and Senior Gear Editor Ryan Brower, have put countless options through the paces in multiple workout types to evaluate their fit, durability, comfort, sound quality, and price throughout all of it.

The testing environments included, but weren't limited to, busy CrossFit boxes, elite performance facilities, mountain trails, garage gyms, and outdoor tracks. We even wrote this story while testing various models to listen to podcasts, tunes, and even books in a busy work environment.

The guide below highlights what we believe are the best workout headphones to know about right now.

Sweat causes a lot of wear and tear on over-ear headphones, especially the ear pads. We recommend using only over-ear headphones for moderate activity, such as lifting and light cardio. You don't really want to wear over-ear headphones for HIIT or other high-cardio workouts, because the more sweat you create, the more likely your headphones are to slip and slide around on your head.

Over-ear headphones do provide great sound quality, and if you are strategic about how you work out with them, you likely won't see sweat as an issue until much farther down the road. In one specific case, one of our editors was a big fan of his Beats Studio Pro headphones for weight lifting. It took about 3 years for his ear pads to wear down from sweat and regular use, so he then bought replacement ear pads for those headphones.

You won't have any problems with sweat and water resistance on the running headphones we curated above; however, some are rated higher than others—which means they're more durable.

When you see an "IP" rating, what you are really reading is the Ingress Protection rating. You can read a rating from each number: the first indicates dust protection, and the second indicates water protection. Some of the headphones above have an "X" as the first number. The X indicates that no data were collected on dust protection. Sometimes it helps to know the dust protection, especially if you're running on trails. If you're a trail runner, it's probably worth paying attention to the more rugged running headphones that also have a dust rating.

The second number indicates how waterproof the headphones are. Zero is the lowest, while 9K is the highest (you're unlikely to see a 9K rating on headphones because this level is reserved for materials that can handle direct, high-temp, high-pressure water).

So what's a good number range to look for? We recommend anything between four and six, but seven and higher is a premium rating and can withstand full submersion in water. Most of what you will see is "IPX4", which is solid, but if you're really wanting a pair of running headphones that are made to handle high activity, then look for a second number of seven or above.

Eligible asset managers, ETF issuers, and other institutional clients will be able to stake supported assets held with BNY.

Banking giant BNY will use Figment's validator infrastructure to support its staking offering, allowing eligible institutions to stake assets through the bank's existing Digital Asset Custody service.

Asset managers, exchange-traded fund issuers, corporates, and wealth management firms are among the staking clients BNY will serve, Figment said Thursday. "Offering staking with Figment in a trusted custody framework can give institutions greater confidence in how they participate, while supporting the standards of security, oversight, and accountability they expect."

BNY launched its digital asset custody service in October 2022, initially accepting bitcoin and ether from select clients. Last month, BNY and Ripple expanded their custody agreement to cover XRP and RLUSD and also announced plans for BNY clients to mint and redeem RLUSD through the bank.

Coinbase Prime first integrated Figment's Ethereum Ethereum (ETH) staking service in early 2024. The partnership had enabled more than $2 billion in staked assets by October 2025, the firms said when they announced support for more networks, including Solana Solana (SOL), Cardano, and Sui.

"Figment is bringing its validator infrastructure and staking expertise to BNY's Digital Asset Custody platform, giving institutional clients another way to participate in staking using their existing platform and servicing workflows," Figment Head of Revenue Josh Deems said.

Figment says it provides staking services to more than 1,500 institutions, including asset managers, custodians, exchanges, and wallets.

#5110. SoFiUSD goes live on Polygon as bank-issued stablecoin expands reach

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The bank-issued stablecoin joins USDC, USDT, and PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

SoFiUSD is now live on Polygon, giving SoFi’s bank-issued stablecoin another blockchain for payments and settlement as the fintech expands its digital asset business.

In a statement shared with The Block on Thursday, Polygon Labs said the stablecoin is available on Polygon Open Money Stack, adding to existing Ethereum and Solana support. Polygon Labs said SoFi’s more than 17 million members can buy, sell, hold, and convert SoFiUSD on Polygon through the SoFi app.

SoFiUSD is issued by SoFi Bank, N.A., an OCC-regulated national bank, and is redeemable one-to-one for U.S. dollars. It joins Circle's USDC, Tether's USDT, and PayPal's PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

According to the statement, the integration gives SoFiUSD access to wallets, fiat rails, stablecoin conversion, and cross-chain settlement on a single interface, allowing customers to use the asset without the need for a separate wallet or manual bridging.

“SoFi is well positioned to bring stablecoins into mainstream finance because it combines the regulatory foundation of a national bank with the reach of a major consumer and enterprise technology platform,” Polygon Labs CEO Marc Boiron said.

The development is the latest in a series of crypto initiatives from SoFi this year. Last month, the company reported 503,000 crypto products at the end of the third quarter, while gross crypto transaction revenue reached $161.6 million, up 20% from the prior quarter.

SoFi also launched its tokenized deposit product in October, allowing customers to convert deposits into SoFiUSD at a one-to-one rate. In September, the bank began settling card transactions using its stablecoin on Mastercard’s network, weeks after agreeing to connect its SoFi Exchange Network with Kraken’s infrastructure.

#5109. Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin is flirting with a historical first that bulls probably don’t want to hear: Two consecutive years of losses, something the cryptocurrency has never experienced in its 18-year history.

The world's largest cryptocurrency finished 2025 down 6.34% and has lost another 15.65% so far in 2026, trading around $73,850 on November 10. If Bitcoin fails to recover above its annual opening price of roughly $87,500 before December 31, it will close two calendar years in the red for the first time.

Historical annual returns show Bitcoin has experienced several brutal crashes since its creation, but each negative calendar year has been followed by a positive one. Even the disastrous 2018 and 2022 bear markets failed to produce consecutive annual losses.

The historical pattern is clear. Its significance, however, deserves a closer look.

Bitcoin's previous annual crashes were considerably more painful than its current losing streak. The cryptocurrency plunged 73.56% in 2018 and another 64.27% in 2022, wiping out most of investors' holdings in each respective year.

By comparison, Bitcoin's 6.34% decline in 2025 was relatively mild. Add the roughly 16% loss recorded this year, and the combined decline comes to approximately 21% after accounting for compounding.

That's a fraction of the losses investors endured in previous crypto winters, despite the market potentially recording a longer sequence of negative annual returns.

Bitcoin's performance in 2024 also provides some perspective. The cryptocurrency surged more than 120% that year, meaning investors who bought at the beginning of 2024 would still be sitting on substantial gains despite the subsequent decline.

The picture changes considerably when measuring losses from the market's actual peak instead of calendar-year boundaries.

Bitcoin reached approximately $126,000 in October 2025 before crashing below $58,000 during 2026. That represented a decline exceeding 50%, a significantly more painful move than the 21% combined loss reflected in the two annual candlesticks.

Analysts have pointed to Bitcoin's increasingly institutional investor base, spot exchange-traded funds, and deeper market liquidity as potential explanations for its comparatively smaller bear-market drawdowns.

Those structural changes may help explain why Bitcoin's current market cycle looks different from previous ones, although they have not eliminated major price crashes.

There is also a distinction between the length of a losing streak and the severity of the losses. A market can decline slowly over several calendar years without inflicting the same damage as a single catastrophic year.

And Bitcoin has already demonstrated that a bad year doesn't necessarily translate into a prolonged downturn. The cryptocurrency gained roughly 92% in 2019 following its 2018 collapse and surged more than 155% in 2023 after its disastrous 2022 performance.

For now, Bitcoin has 50 days left in 2026 to avoid its first back-to-back annual losses. A third consecutive decline in 2027 would extend the streak further, but that scenario is still hypothetical.

At its November 10 price of approximately $73,850, Bitcoin would need to gain about 18.5% to recover its 2026 opening price and finish the year in positive territory.

The launch follows an earlier integration between Ethena Labs and Securitize to offer 24/7 atomic swaps between BlackRock's tokenized fund and Ethena's stablecoin.

Securitize and Ethena Labs have launched a $100 million, 24/7 liquidity facility for BlackRock's USD Institutional Digital Liquidity Fund (BUIDL). It will allow eligible investors to exchange shares of the tokenized fund for supported stablecoins, including USDC, outside traditional market hours.

#5100. The Guy’s Guide to Adult Acne

We’re all stressed AF. The food we’re eating probably isn’t helping. Everyone told us we wouldn’t have to worry about our hormones going crazy after our teen years. What the heck is going on?

THE YEAR WAS 1997. I’d just made the varsity baseball team as a freshman, and I was ready to shoot my shot with every senior girl who’d give me the time of day. The only problem: I had also hard-launched my pepperoni face. I’m not talking surface-level New York slice stuff—these breakouts were Detroit-style-pizza deep. Every clogged pore had clocked in at the same time, and I was an absolute wreck.

For six months, I’d burned through everything the CVS aisles had to offer: Oxy Pads, Stridex, Noxzema, whatever stuff was hawking a promise of clear skin. Nothing worked. My face just got redder, drier, and more irritated. My mom (thank god for her) dragged me to the dermatologist, and I walked out with a prescription for isotretinoin, a.k.a. Accutane. Five months later, my skin was completely clear. My mini CVS stash went into the trash, and I thought that’d be the end of it.

I was wrong. I spent years assuming that because I took Accutane as a teenager, I’d paid my dues, but as a 44-year-old dude, I still get the occasional pimple or two—seemingly right before a big work meeting or whenever I have a lot of stuff on my plate. And it still messes with my head. Does my hygiene suck? Are my hormones to blame? Am I sweating too much? Apparently, I’m not the only one.

According to Christopher Bunick, MD, PhD, an associate professor of dermatology at the Yale School of Medicine, 83 percent of teen boys will deal with acne before they reach their 20s, which is often due to a surge in testosterone at the time. And while acne typically clears up as testosterone levels stabilize in our 20s, it can make a surprise comeback in our 30s, 40s, 50s, and beyond—with around 20 percent of men in their 30s dealing with breakouts, but only 12 percent seeking treatment from a medical professional. It’s not just a skin issue: Research shows that adult acne is consistently linked to a higher risk of developing anxiety, depression, and reduced self-esteem.

Acne (not to be mistaken for folliculitis, which can result from shaving) is mainly caused by a combination of excess oil production and dead skin cells that clog pores. This can create an environment where strains of Cutibacterium acnes—the bacteria naturally found on your skin—become imbalanced and trigger inflammation. This could look like blackheads and whiteheads, raised pustules and papules, or the deeper, more painful nodules or cysts that are more likely to scar. And despite what your instincts tell you, excessively scrubbing your face won’t help—it could actually harm your skin barrier. “You cannot wash away hormones,” says Dr. Bunick. Acne is an inflammatory condition, not a hygiene problem.

Scientists still aren’t sure exactly what makes some guys more susceptible to acne than others, but genetics and hormones are two leading factors. Research suggests about 80 percent of your acne risk is inherited. Your genes determine how sensitive your skin is to the hormones (i.e., androgens) that regulate the sebaceous glands and how aggressively your immune system overreacts to fight the inflammation. Then there are the lifestyle factors that play a smaller role but can collectively add up, such as diet (Dr. Bunick is a believer in the gut-skin axis—the idea that an imbalanced gut microbiome can drive systemic inflammation that can predispose you to acne), stress (this spikes cortisol, which also drives oil production and contributes to inflammation), sleep deprivation (same), and testosterone replacement therapy.

Though there isn’t a cure for acne yet, there are plenty of treatment options. “Untreated acne doesn’t just affect your skin. It affects how you move through the world,” says Dr. Bunick. “Acne impacts confidence, whether it’s making friends, having a social or dating life, or performance in work, sports, or other extracurriculars.” Earlier intervention means less of a psychosocial toll, less scarring, and (you heard the man) more confidence.

So if your bathroom still looks like mine did in the ’90s, it’s time to investigate what might be causing the chaos in your largest organ and learn how to treat it. The stories below will help you get your skin clear and, hopefully, keep it that way.

Bob Gearing is a New York-based writer covering men’s grooming from the perspective of a very real (and very sweaty) dude. By day, he’s a well-SPF’d tech sales bro; by night, he’s a red-light mask guy who definitely didn’t think he’d care this much about skincare. When he’s not testing the latest face serum, you’ll find him sweating through a round of golf or holding out hope that the Lions will make the Super Bowl before he dies.

#5099. DTCC plans to bring tokenized securities to Solana in second half of 2027

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The planned Solana integration follows the launch of DTCC's tokenization service on Canton Network and its private Besu network last month.

The Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation announced Thursday it plans to bring tokenized versions of eligible securities held by its Depository Trust Company subsidiary to Solana Solana (SOL) in the second half of 2027, connecting its DTCC Tokenization Service to the Layer 1 blockchain.

The service went live on Oct. 28, initially on Canton Network and DTCC's private Besu network. Eligible users can convert their securities into tokenized versions and back again, with delivery to digital wallets of their choice. DTCC said at launch that it planned to add further blockchain networks next year.

"DTC-tokenized assets made available on Solana – and all blockchain networks integrated with the service – will retain the same investor protections, ownership rights and regulatory safeguards that apply to traditionally held securities," Brian Steele, president of DTCC Clearing & Securities Services, said in the announcement.

DTC received a Securities and Exchange Commission no-action letter last December for the tokenization service, allowing DTC to tokenize Russell 1000 stocks, major U.S. equity index exchange-traded funds, and U.S. Treasury bills, bonds, and notes.

DTCC's first limited production trades of tokenized stocks and Treasurys began in July on Canton and Besu, with JPMorgan tokenizing part of its Invesco QQQ Trust holdings held at DTCC in the trial. The bank retained the ability to convert them back to traditional shares.

DTCC named 20 participants that had signed agreements to use the service at its October launch, including Bank of America, Citi, JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, and UBS. DTCC said its depository subsidiary provided custody and asset servicing for $114 trillion in securities in 2025.

#5096. Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Five years after it first floated the idea, Germany’s largest lender is now holding Bitcoin for clients.

Deutsche Bank said Wednesday that its Digital Asset Custody service has gone live for paying clients, giving institutional and corporate customers in Europe a regulated way to store and transfer Bitcoin, Ethereum and three stablecoins: Circle’s USDC and EURC, plus EURAU.

The bank manages wallets and private keys on clients’ behalf. For now, the service covers custody and transfers, with tokenized financial instruments on its roadmap.

The service is aimed at clients of Deutsche Bank’s corporate and investment banking units, including corporations, asset managers, hedge funds, custodians, brokers and sovereign institutions, according to the bank’s September announcement of the plan.

Its setup includes hardware-based key protection, multi-person approvals and separate warm and cold storage, the bank said.

“In the medium term we aim to deliver a fully integrated, regulated ecosystem that supports the custody, tokenisation, and settlement of digital assets,” said Paul Maley, Deutsche Bank’s global head of trust and securities services.

Deutsche Bank also signed a memorandum of understanding with Deutsche Asset Management, the majority-owned money manager formerly known as DWS, to explore working together on tokenized money market funds, crypto exchange-traded commodities and stablecoins.

The asset manager oversaw about 1.22 trillion euros as of Sept. 30. The bank has also said it plans to expand the custody service to Asia.

The launch caps a long road, going back to at least 2021 when Deutsche Bank began readying a crypto custody and trading platform. Then, in 2023, the bank applied for a digital asset custody license in Germany. Last year, reports said the bank planned to build the service with technology from crypto exchange Bitpanda, alongside its longtime partner Taurus.

Deutsche Bank received authorization from German regulator BaFin under the EU’s Markets in Crypto-Assets regulation, or MiCA, which requires crypto service providers to be licensed. The bank cautioned that its custody service doesn’t protect clients against swings in the value of digital assets, fraud or failures by other market participants.

The custody launch isn’t Deutsche Bank’s only crypto bet. The bank is among a group of global lenders, including Goldman Sachs and Citi, backing a $270 million challenge to crypto’s dominant stablecoin issuers.

#5094. Andreessen Horowitz, DeFi Education Fund push back against Illinois crypto tax

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

A crypto cohort filed an amicus brief backing the crypto industry's challenge to Illinois's Digital Asset Tax Act on Thursday.

Crypto industry heavyweights are banding together to challenge Illinois over its controversial crypto tax, with the DeFi Education Fund and Andreessen Horowitz among those joining the fight.

On Thursday, the group filed an amicus brief backing the crypto industry's challenge to the Digital Asset Tax Act in Illinois, arguing that the law is overly broad and unfairly targets the digital asset industry.

"The Act singles out transactions involving blockchain technology for unfavorable tax treatment and affects a substantial number of Illinois residents: an estimated 2.64 million Illinoisans hold digital assets, the fifth-highest total of any state," they said in the brief.

The DeFi Education Fund, Paradigm, Uniswap and Andreessen Horowitz led the entities that signed the letter, which include  Aave Labs, Aptos Labs, Aztec Labs, Chainlink Labs, the Decentralization Research Center, Dromos Labs, Figment Inc., Hyperliquid Policy Center, Jito Labs, Multicoin Capital Management LLC, Orca Creative, Phantom Technologies, Solana Policy Institute and Stand With Crypto.

The filing adds to a growing legal battle over the legislation. The Blockchain Association, the Crypto Council for Innovation and The Digital Chamber have sued Illinois after its governor, JB Pritzker, signed into law the Digital Asset Tax Act as part of the state's fiscal year 2027 budget planning. The law imposes a 0.2% tax on digital asset transactions and was initially scheduled to go into effect in January 2027, but has since been postponed to July 2027.

The law has drawn swift criticism from crypto industry advocates, who have described it as unprecedented and sought to block its implementation. In the amicus brief on Thursday, the group said the law does not take into account the "technological complexities" that come with digital assets.

"The Act fails to establish sufficiently clear boundaries between taxable digital asset business activity and the provision of software and network infrastructure that enables users to transact independently," they said.

#5091. Tether Gold issuer follows USDT to complete Big Four audit

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Tether said KPMG U.S. inspected every gold bar backing XAUT, following the USDT issuer's first full financial audit in August.

Tether said Thursday that KPMG U.S. completed an independent audit of the financial statements of TG Commodities, the issuer of its gold-backed XAUT token.

A spokesperson for the Big Four firm subsequently confirmed to The Block that it issued an unqualified, or "clean," opinion on TG Commodities's financial statements in accordance with AICPA standards for the year ending Dec. 31, 2025.

In a blog post, Tether said KPMG physically inspected and counted every individual gold bar backing XAUT Tether Gold (XAUT), checking the identifying information on each one. As of Dec. 31, 2025, TG Commodities held 520,089.350 fine troy ounces of gold worth $2.246 billion, against 520,089.300 XAUT in circulation, according to the firm.

The gold belongs to token holders and is stored in Switzerland. Each XAUT represents "an undivided ownership interest in gold on specified bars," Tether said.

KPMG had already audited the 2025 accounts of USDT Tether (USDT) stablecoin issuer Tether International. Tether announced that audit in August, and a spokesperson for the accounting firm confirmed to The Block that it had issued an unqualified opinion. Before then, Tether's regular reserve reporting consisted of attestations covering its holdings and liabilities on a given date.

"Our strategy was deliberate: start with the largest and most complex businesses, USDT and XAUT, and set the standard for the industry," Tether Chief Financial Officer Simon McWilliams said on Thursday.

The company is working toward audits of the rest of its businesses, and the audited figures matched Tether's quarterly reserve reports, he said.

#5089. The Paths to Clear(er) Skin

With a little trust and patience, it’s possible to find a solution that works for you. These three guys are proof.

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

IT’S ONE THING to get advice from a doctor (still important and invaluable), but it’s another to hear from other guys who’ve been there. Guys who understand what it’s like to cancel plans because of a whopper of a zit. Guys who’ve also hesitated to take their shirts off at the pool for fear of being judged for their bacne. We wanted to hear about their struggles, and successes—what finally worked to help them send breakouts packing and regain their confidence. As you’ll learn from these three men, no two plans are the same. But with a little consistency and some experimentation, blemish-free skin is possible.

I like to take care of myself. I work out at least five times a week. I’m consistent with my diet. But my confidence takes a hit because of my acne. Since I was a teenager, I’ve dealt with deep cystic breakouts that have totally upended my life. I’ve canceled dates; I’ve canceled trips; I won’t leave the house when I have a flare. It’s improved as I’ve gotten older—since 2023 I’ve been using Winlevi, a prescription hormone-blocking cream, in the morning and at night, and Tazorac, a doctor-dispensed retinoid that I’ve been using since my 20s, at night—but I still deal with the occasional breakout. And it still messes with my confidence.

I went to Greece over the summer, and I could feel a big one coming under my skin. The one thing that really makes a difference is the La Roche-Posay Effaclar Duo+M treatment, which you can get for $25 at the drugstore. I apply it religiously every few hours as soon as I feel a zit coming on, and sometimes it helps it come to a head faster or stops it from forming altogether. I’ve found a routine that works, but acne is always something lingering in the back of my mind. —Derek Monacchio, 38, tax director

Back acne is something I’ve had my entire life, but honestly, I just thought it was something I’d always have to deal with. I couldn’t reach back there with a cream or a wash, and I didn’t want to go on Accutane or any prescription medications. Even though most people couldn’t see it, I’d get very self-conscious whenever I’d take my shirt off in public, which sucked because I take good care of my body.

That all changed when I went in for a laser hair removal appointment at a physician-led laser center, The Kaga Institute, about six months ago. The office staff knew my situation with the breakouts on my back and told me they had something that could help—a new laser called AviClear, which supposedly suppresses oil gland production. I was curious, so I gave it a try. After three sessions, the pimples on my back were gone. It was totally painless for me—especially when compared to the laser hair removal I’ve tried. Each click of the laser felt like a warm sensation for a millisecond, and I was able to continue with my laser hair removal at the same time. Now that I’ve finished my series of treatments, I was told the results should improve with time. It feels great to have found a solution that works. I’m ready for next summer already. —William Sebok, 42, construction company owner

There’s a perception that when you have acne, you find a solution and then it’s over. That has not been my experience. My first breakouts (a mix of whiteheads and inflamed red bumps) started just as I was hitting puberty, around age 11, and persisted well into my 30s. I’ve seen a few different dermatologists since I was 15 and was prescribed everything under the sun—from clindamycin (a prescription antibiotic) to Accutane. I would take the medications for a few months at a time, and some would help for a while, but once I stopped, my skin would inevitably break out again. Plus, I experienced some side effects from the Accutane, including significant lethargy.

After I stopped taking the medications, I started looking at the labels of the skin care products I was using and noticed that when the ingredient list was extremely long, there was a higher likelihood there would be something in there that would clog my pores. [Editor’s note: Look for the label “noncomedogenic,” a.k.a. not pore clogging, on your products.] For me, it became more about avoiding certain products and soothing the skin barrier. I also became much more careful about not overloading my routine with too many strong ingredients. I focused instead on making sure the products I was using were appropriate for my acne-prone skin. That was the game changer. Another thing that causes my skin to react is my diet. If I’m eating a lot of high-glycemic foods and meals that are high in sugar, I immediately break out. I also try to wash my face or shower immediately after every workout, and if I can’t, I spray on a hypochlorous acid spray to kill the bacteria. Less is more, and I’m proof of that. —Brute Choi, 37, content creator

Brian Underwood is beauty director at Women’s Health, where he oversees content strategy for the brand across all platforms, including digital, print, and social. Underwood previously served as beauty and wellness director at Oprah Daily and O, The Oprah Magazine. During his tenure leading beauty content for the Oprah brand at Hearst, stories Underwood commissioned were awarded the Skin Cancer Foundation Media Award and a Fragrance Award for Editorial Excellence (his second). He was the launch Beauty Director of Dr. Oz THE GOOD LIFE, and has held additional editorial positions at Fitness, Organic Style, Good Housekeeping, Life & Style Weekly, and Woman’s Day and has written for Self, Shape, Seventeen, Redbook, Cosmopolitan, and many more. Underwood previously served on the Skin Cancer Foundation’s gala committee and as partnerships director of the Trans Beauty Clinic, a New York-based charitable organization that provided beauty services and workshops to the city’s trans community.

#5087. Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether, the company behind the world's largest stablecoin, USDT, is getting surprisingly good at making tiny AI models punch above their weight.

The company on Wednesday unveiled QVAC BotPsy, an 800-million-parameter AI agent designed to carry out everyday tasks on personal devices. Parameters are essentially the knobs that determine how much an AI model can do, and more of them generally translate to improved performance. And for reference, frontier AI models today run in the trillions of parameters.

In the company's own testing, Tether’s relatively tiny model outperformed competitors with up to 2.5 times as many parameters, suggesting that AI assistants may not need enormous models to get things done.

Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, BotPsy is agentic, meaning it’s designed to take action. It can identify which tools an application needs, execute commands in the right order, and adjust when something goes wrong.

For example, Tether described a benchmark scenario in which an AI assistant needed to send a message but encountered two problems: Wi-Fi was disabled and the inbox was full. BotPsy turned on Wi-Fi, cleared enough storage, and completed the task, while the underlying model repeatedly attempted the same failed operation.

That's a fairly mundane achievement, but it's precisely the kind of problem that makes AI agents useful outside a chatbot window.

BotPsy is built on Alibaba's Qwen3.5-0.8B architecture, a relatively small language model that Tether trained further to specialize in interacting with software tools. Its 800 million parameters represent the numerical values the model learned during training, and a smaller parameter count generally means less memory is needed to run it.

According to Tether's technical documentation, BotPsy scored 44.0% on BFCL v4, a benchmark that evaluates an AI system's ability to select and execute software functions across different tasks and conversations. Scores reflect a weighted percentage of successful performance across tool-use categories.

The underlying Qwen3.5-0.8B model scored 23.4%, while the considerably larger Qwen3.5-2B reached 37.7%.

BotPsy also scored 58.7% on ACEBench, which evaluates the accuracy of function calls, including selecting the appropriate tools and arguments. Its base model managed 30.1%, while the 2-billion-parameter competitor scored 55.4%.

The company tested five models across six tool-use evaluations, with BotPsy posting the highest results on five. Its average score across those tests reached 45.0%, compared with 38.8% for the larger Qwen model.

BotPsy is the latest addition to QVAC, Tether's effort to build an AI ecosystem that runs on devices controlled by users rather than relying entirely on centralized cloud infrastructure.

The company first introduced QVAC in May 2025, positioning the project as an alternative to AI services operated by technology giants. Its longer-term ambitions included autonomous agents capable of interacting with applications and potentially making payments using Bitcoin and USDT.

Tether expanded that initiative in October 2025 with a 41-billion-token dataset for training science and engineering models, alongside QVAC Workbench, an application that lets users run AI models locally.

Earlier this year, Tether released its SDK, providing developers with tools to build offline AI applications across mobile and desktop operating systems.

The company also developed QVAC MedPsy, a medical AI system designed to run on consumer hardware. Its 1.7-billion-parameter version outperformed Google's much larger MedGemma-27B on HealthBench Hard, a test of realistic clinical conversations evaluated using physician-developed criteria.

BotPsy is currently available as an open-weight model under the Apache 2.0 license, meaning developers can download, modify, and integrate it into their own applications. Tether has also released compressed versions intended to reduce memory requirements for local deployment.

The company has not announced a release date for a finished consumer assistant powered by BotPsy. Its published model card also says the training code and training data are not included in the release.

Unocoin says it lacks full visibility into its frozen BitGo wallets and estimates a server rebuild will take up to five working days.

Indian cryptocurrency exchange Unocoin has identified "outgoing transactions" from an ETH address in its BitGo wallet infrastructure, adding a new development to a security incident that has left customers unable to access their accounts since Nov. 9.

In its latest statement, the exchange said it discovered the activity on Thursday and immediately escalated the matter to BitGo, which has opened an investigation.

The exchange said the incident traces back to Nov. 6, when it first detected unauthorized activity on its infrastructure and initiated "precautionary measures'' to protect user funds.

"As part of these measures, we requested an immediate freeze of our enterprise accounts with BitGo and Fireblocks, which hold cryptocurrency balances required to facilitate our day-to-day crypto transactions," the exchange wrote.

The incident took another turn on Monday, when the exchange said it detected more unauthorized activity on its platform and suspended all services immediately in the "interest of protecting customer assets and data." This included implementing what it described as "login suspension."

Unocoin says more than 90% of customer cryptocurrency is held in cold storage and that no funds were lost in the incident. However, when asked by a user on X whether those could be recovered, the exchange replied that it would not "give you an assurance based on assumptions" until transaction-level details are verified.

The exchange also said it cannot log in to the frozen accounts and that BitGo's use of change addresses limits what it can check on-chain.

"We currently do not have complete visibility into all potentially unintended transactions across our BitGo wallets," it wrote in the incident report.

Unocoin said rebuilding its servers from the ground up would take an estimated four to five working days. The investigation into the breach, potential data exposure, and status of user holdings remains ongoing.

Meanwhile, Onchain Lens reported that Arkham shows $0 across 81,000 tracked Unocoin addresses and that the exchange's website returned a 504 error. The exchange has not confirmed that its tracked wallets were drained.

#5084. Can Acne Be Prevented With a Vaccine?

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

WHAT IF ACNE wasn’t so damn frustrating to treat or just…didn’t exist at all? George Liu, MD, PhD, and a team of researchers at the University of California, San Diego, are developing a vaccine designed to target certain types of bacteria to help prevent acne from forming in the first place.

Dr. Liu’s team researches how bacterial pathogens cause diseases. While doing so, they discovered that C. acnes—a bacteria that naturally lives on the skin and whose imbalance is a known acne trigger—secretes two forms of an enzyme called hyaluronidase: Hyl-A and Hyl-B. Hyl-A plays a central role in the development of acne by causing inflammation, whereas Hyl-B is an anti-inflammatory that can be beneficial for skin by reducing inflammation and helping prevent an aggressive immune response.

Existing acne treatments are broad and often focus on killing all C. acnes strains, which can take several months and introduce significant side effects that can range anywhere from dryness, redness, peeling, and irritation to antibiotic resistance. Instead, Dr. Liu’s vaccine—which has been tested only on rodents so far—aims to teach the immune system to block just Hyl-A, leaving the benefits of Hyl-B and preventing inflammatory acne from forming in the first place.

UC San Diego isn’t the only group chasing an acne vaccine. Paris-based pharmaceutical company Sanofi is developing an mRNA vaccine to treat active breakouts rather than prevent future ones. “The idea is to target two of the key contributors to this inflammatory cycle—the [C. acnes] bacterial strains that cause inflammation and the associated toxins—and aim to halt the disease process,” says Jean-François Toussaint, PhD, global head of vaccine research and development at Sanofi. The vaccine is currently being tested in adults with moderate to severe acne during a phase I/II clinical trial.

These vaccines could potentially do wonders for chronic acne sufferers and help save hundreds of dollars per year on acne products and medications. Between his team’s research and Sanofi’s developments, Dr. Liu predicts an acne vaccine could be available to the public within the next five years.

Sean Zucker is the Grooming and Reviews Editor at Men's Health. He brings over half a decade of experience covering health, wellness, and lifestyle. When Sean's not writing about hair products, you can find him running for exercise, or toward the nearest food truck.

#5082. This New AI Music Model Is Already Better Than Suno—We Put ACE to the Test

This New AI Music Model Is Already Better Than Suno—We Put ACE to the Test

Artificial intelligence has gotten pretty good at writing songs. Now, a relatively unfamiliar competitor has managed to beat Suno, arguably the biggest name in AI music, at its own game.

ACE Music v1, developed by ACE Studio, has taken first place on Artificial Analysis' vocal music leaderboard, scoring 1,160 Elo points against Suno v6's 1,144.

The AA-Music-Vocal v1.1 benchmark measures the quality of AI-generated songs containing vocals. Evaluators listen to tracks produced from identical prompts without knowing which model created them, then choose their favorite. Those preferences determine an Elo-style rating, with higher scores indicating stronger listener preference.

The lead is relatively narrow, and the models' statistical confidence intervals overlap. Suno also retains the top position on the separate instrumental music leaderboard.

ACE Studio, founded in 2018, originally focused on synthesizing singing voices from MIDI notes and lyrics. Its latest ACE Music v1 model generates complete songs from written descriptions or user-provided lyrics.

In Decrypt's hands-on tests, ACE Music consistently produced more melodic and coherent songs in mainstream styles.

That said, the vocal performances sounded more natural in general, with fewer of the synthetic artifacts that sometimes make AI singers sound like they're struggling to pronounce their own lyrics, use a lot of autotune or hallucinate words just to fill spaces. (Go ahead and give our song a listen here, which incidentally is about our latest company announcement regarding the Information Exchange.)

ACE also handled song endings better. Where Suno occasionally introduces strange sounds, unnecessary repetitions, hallucinations or abrupt musical changes near the finish, ACE generally maintained its structure and delivered cleaner conclusions.

That matters because making a convincing chorus is relatively easy compared with maintaining a musical idea throughout an entire composition even if the lyrics are already over.

Suno still had an advantage when we pushed the models toward less conventional genres.

In our melodic death metal test, ACE consistently produced tracks featuring a woman singing clearly in a pleasant, clean voice, missing the aggressive vocal delivery expected from the requested style. In general it confuses metal genres all over. Suno offered more creative arrangements in the proper heavy styles, with better stylistic consistency in several similarly demanding prompts.

The results were subjective rather than a controlled benchmark, but they showed why a leaderboard victory doesn't automatically make one model better for every musician.

ACE's most interesting feature may be its songwriting workflow.

Its “Idea” feature can develop a basic concept into lyrics and a structured composition, identifying individual song sections and describing how they should be arranged. Users can then refine the material rather than repeatedly generating completely new tracks.

The v1.1 Preview goes further, creating a musical score before generating audio.

“Each generation is a new performance, so the sound may differ even when the score stays the same,” the developers explain.

Users can edit melodies and chords, regenerate performances, export MIDI files and separate vocals from instruments for further production.

That workflow puts ACE closer to a music production assistant than a simple prompt-to-song application.

The flagship ACE Music v1 model remains closed-source with no public API, so users can only generate via the official site. However, its developers are pursuing a parallel open-source strategy.

ACE Studio and StepFun have already released ACE-Step 1.5, a separate family of models whose code and weights are publicly available. Unlike the proprietary flagship, these can run on users' own hardware—but using a weaker model.

The company also promises another release.

“We plan to open-source ACE Music v1 Flash in late 2026,” the developers state on their website, describing a version designed to balance quality, generation speed and hardware requirements.

ACE Music is available through its browser application, with a limited free tier and paid subscriptions. Commercial usage rights for flagship-model songs require a paid plan, while ACE-Step offers a separate free generation option.

For now, ACE Music v1 holds a 16-point Elo lead over Suno v6 in vocal generation. Its promised Flash release would give local users another model to try before the end of 2026.

#5079. Visa adds Western Union’s USDPT stablecoin to its settlement network in Anchorage tie-up

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Visa and eligible partners can now settle VisaNet obligations using Western Union's USDPT, issued by Anchorage Digital Bank.

Anchorage Digital said it is collaborating with Visa to support select stablecoins issued by its federally chartered bank as native settlement assets on the Visa network, starting with Western Union's USDPT.

Visa and eligible partners can use the token to settle VisaNet obligations, with the companies also creating a framework to consider additional Anchorage-issued stablecoins, according to a statement shared with The Block.

"Stablecoins have made money move faster, but speed alone won't bring institutions onchain," Anchorage Digital CEO Nathan McCauley said. "They need a regulated institution behind the money. With Anchorage Digital select issued stablecoins now being supported on the Visa network, institutions get both: the speed of a stablecoin and the safety of a federally regulated bank behind it."

USDPT debuted in May on Solana, with Anchorage Digital Bank handling issuance and Fireblocks providing the wallet and settlement infrastructure. Western Union initially launched the stablecoin in the Philippines and Bolivia, with plans to use it for 24/7 settlements across its network of more than 200 countries.

Anchorage has also issued stablecoins for Tether (USAT), OSL Group (USDGO), Ethena Labs (USDtb), Falcon Finance (fUSD), and Zelle (ZLUSD).

Anchorage reached a valuation of more than $3 billion in its $350 million Series D funding round in 2021. It has spent this year expanding its institutional services, including onchain lending through Spark and custody-based collateral management.

Anchorage said the partnership with Visa fits its broader push to become a settlement bank for stablecoins, extending its business beyond issuance into custody and settlement services for institutions.

The partnership comes as stablecoin supply contracts. According to The Block's data dashboard, the current total supply has fallen to around $280 billion, down from $300 billion in March this year. Tether's USDT dominates the sector with $177 billion, followed by Circle's USDC at around $70 billion.

For Visa, the Anchorage partnership builds on an expanding stablecoin settlement network. The company said in September that its annualized stablecoin settlement run rate reached $20 billion, more than 15 times the year-earlier figure.

Visa has also added networks including Monad, Canton, Base, and Tempo to its settlement options.
