#5078. Is TRT Causing Your Acne?

Guys who take higher doses of testosterone are at a greater risk of developing acne, but it can happen at any dose.

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

THERE ARE PLENTY of reasons to like testosterone replacement therapy (TRT)—it can boost energy, increase muscle mass, and improve sex drive—but there’s one potentially annoying side effect: acne, or, uh, bacne (acne on your back and shoulders). TRT can increase sebaceous gland activity, which boosts oil production that can clog pores, trap bacteria, and cause breakouts. The areas around the back and shoulders, along with the chest, have a high concentration of androgen-responsive oil glands.

When the total testosterone in your body increases, it gets converted to dihydro­testosterone (DHT), a major androgen, and estrogen. DHT ramps up oil production in the skin, according to Akhil Muthigi, MD, a urologist at Houston Methodist Hospital. This can lead to inflammatory acne (papules, pustules, nodules, and cysts) though whiteheads and blackheads are common as well. Dr. Muthigi says that guys who take higher doses of TRT (anything near or over 200 milligrams per week) are at a greater risk of developing acne, but it can happen at any dose. It largely depends on your skin biology (i.e., the amount of androgen receptors on your skin that trigger sebaceous gland growth and, therefore, oil production), as well as genetic factors.

TRT treatments that provide steadier levels (such as daily creams and gels) are less likely to cause acne than other forms that cause spikes (such as injections), says Danny Guo, MD, a cosmetic and surgical dermatologist. He adds that if you start TRT and see acne forming within a few weeks to a few months of taking it, stopping the therapy won’t necessarily eliminate the acne. So it’s best to talk to a dermatologist before bailing on the therapy altogether. Fortunately, TRT-triggered acne is as treatable as any form, with the strategies here proving effective.

#5077. Everyone Is Capable of Having Multiple Orgasms. Sex Experts Explain How

Plus, they share the health benefits of an extended climax.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Pleasure powerfully contributes to overall well-being. If your sex life feels good, it's likely that you feel good, too—emotionally, mentally, physically, and relationally. That's why, especially for women and people with vulvas, having multiple orgasms can be an awesome way to amplify your life in—and out—of the bedroom.

Simply, “multiple orgasms refer to a person having more than one orgasm in a single session, with short or no recovery time in between,” says Shamyra Howard, PhD, CST, LCSW, a sex therapist and author of Use Your Mouth. Just to be clear: “There’s no set or universal number of orgasms a person can achieve in one setting,” she adds. “Some people may experience two or three; others five (or more) in a single session.”

For those with vulvas, multiple orgasms might begin with one climax that immediately blends into the next with little or no notable pause. Sometimes called rolling orgasms, these “can feel like riding wave after wave of feel-good sensation,” says sex and relationship expert Tara Suwinyattichaiporn, PhD, a professor of relational and sexual communication at California State University and author of How Do You Like It?.

Other folks may have a brief comedown period followed by a slower climb back up to arousal before the next orgasm happens. Regardless of the exact rhythm of the orgasms, and whether a person is having two orgasms or ten, the defining feature is continuity. “Multiple orgasms are all about sustaining arousal after the first orgasm’s peak and continuing the pleasure response, instead of shutting it down,” says Howard.

If you think that sounds enjoyable, you’d be correct. But beyond being just plain fun, orgasming multiple times can actually improve your health, says Howard. Each and every orgasm releases feel-good hormones, like dopamine and oxytocin, while simultaneously decreasing levels of cortisol, the stress hormone. Getting multiple doses of those neurochemicals can feel connective, deeply satisfying, and relaxing—even if each climax varies in intensity.

Plus, orgasms release prolactin, a peptide hormone strongly correlated with sleep duration. That post-climax chemical cocktail “really helps you relax and get a much better quality of sleep,” Miriam Sivkin, MD, an ob-gyn in New York, previously told Women’s Health.

But you don’t just benefit from reaching O-town, the journey to get there may also be informative and connective. “[Exploring multiple orgasms] can help people learn their body’s rhythms and increase body awareness,” says Howard. Meaning, you stop as soon as you cross it. With practice, this can help you and your partner(s) engage with pleasure as something that can unfold slowly and even ebb and flow.

However, coming again and again usually comes (heh) easier to vulva-owners because they don’t have the same refractory period—essentially a recovery window—after orgasms as most people with penises. This makes it easier to have another orgasm soon after the first one.

The majority of penis-having people, however, usually need time and distance after an orgasm before pleasure becomes accessible again, she says. Of course, there are no absolutes in sex and pleasure. A penis-owner can train their body to have non-ejaculatory or full-body orgasms—often with the help of breathwork, pelvic floor control, and stimulation—that make multiple orgasms more accessible, says Howard. (If you’ve got questions about the pathway to multiple orgasms for penis-owners, head to this explainer on prostate orgasms, or this one about the male G-spot, as the guide below primarily focuses on making multiple orgasms more accessible for people with vulvas.)

In other words, women’s refractory period is usually marked by a blip of heightened sensitivity, as compared to a full-on stop for many men.

Along your sexual exploration, you may have heard of simultaneous orgasms and blended orgasms. These terms might sound similar, but they describe separate sexperiences.

Multiple Orgasms vs. Simultaneous Orgasms

“Simultaneous orgasms are when two people climax at the same time,” says Howard. With that, two (or more) people are a prerequisite for simultaneous orgasms, whereas multiple orgasms can absolutely occur during a solo session.

Blended orgasms, on the other hand, refer to an orgasm that stems from two or more pleasure centers being stimulated at once—for example, combining clitoral stimulation and vaginal penetration, or clitoral stimulation and anal play.

Sure, both blended and multiple orgasms might be considered amplified versions of the standard O. Still, while blended orgasms are about layering multiple sensations, multiple orgasms involve many separate climaxes, which can all stem from the same type of stimulation repeated.

It all depends on a variety of factors, such as your unique physiology, arousal levels at the time of play, comfort with your own body and your partner, the techniques used, where you are in your menstrual cycle, hydration and stress levels, and more, says Howard. That said, these expert tips can help you set the conditions for multiple orgasms to unfold:

Expectations, as the saying goes, are the thief of joy. And sexually, pressure to perform is the antithesis of arousal. When you focus intensely on achieving orgasm(s), your system shifts into a goal-oriented mode that can make it harder to enjoy the experience—no matter what happens, says Howard.

Research on multiple orgasms is limited, but less than one-fifth of women report having had multiple orgasms in a single session, according to the International Society for Sexual Medicine. So, it might not happen for you, and that’s okay!

Even if you do wind up joining the swath of pleasure-seekers who have had multiple orgasms, there’s no promising it will rock your world. “Each orgasm doesn’t always get more intense; sometimes, the second or third are softer,” says Howard, adding that the point of exploring multiple orgasms isn’t to stack intensity, but to stay tuned into your body.

It is common for people on their multiple orgasm journey to try to get the first orgasm “out of the way,” as if rushing through the first will facilitate the next ones.

Unfortunately, this usually has the opposite effect, says Holly Richmond, PhD, a sex therapist, licensed marriage and family therapist, and author of Reclaiming Pleasure. “Our bodies and nervous systems need to be open and relaxed to achieve multi orgasms.” So, rushing is entirely counterproductive.

Rather than treating orgasm like a yellow light that you need to speed through before it turns red, take your sweet, sweet time building arousal, says Richmond. Let yourself revel in the entire experience, including all the parts and pleasures that preface a (potential) orgasm, she adds.

In practice, this might mean teasing through fabric before stripping naked, making out for eons like teenagers, treating nipple play as the main event, or even just eye-gazing. Some of the most reliable routes to multiple orgasms are likely hanging out (hopefully, charged!) in your bedside drawer. “Sex toys like vibrators and dildos can help you experience and explore a variety of sensations that the human hand and body just aren’t capable of without them,” says Richmond.

Ultimately, which pleasure products you reach for will depend on your specific preferences. But if you’ve got a clitoral stimulator on hand (e.g., air suction toy, rose toy, classic clit vibe, or rabbit), Richmond suggests starting there.

“The majority of women need intense clitoral stimulation to achieve orgasm,” she says. “In fact, only two percent put something in their vagina for self-pleasure. So it makes sense to offer a little help in this area when pursuing multiple orgasms.”

In general, sex toys feel best when used with lube as this helps the toy slide and glide over your genitals. With rose and other suction toys, a dab of lube can even help the toy recreate the sensation of oral sex. Just be sure to use a water-based lubricant with sex toys made from silicone (which most are), as silicone-based lubricant can degrade the material.

“If you’re someone who enjoys anal play, adding a dildo, butt plug, or finger can increase overall arousal and make it easier to stay in the orgasmic zone,” says Richmond, making multiple orgasms possible.

While anal stimulation is often talked about as if it is separate from the rest of sexual pleasure, the nerves in and around the butt are interconnected with those throughout the pelvic floor, she says. Some women report that external anal stimulation (think: rimming, perineal pressure, or anus teasing) can heighten sensation throughout the entire pelvis, helping to maintain arousal.

Further, the vaginal and anal canals are basically next-door neighbors in a condo—meaning, they share a wall. So, anal stimulation can create a feeling of fullness or pressure that indirectly stimulates the vagina and clitoral internal network, which can deepen pleasure and make it easier to stay in the wave between orgasms, says Richmond.

In all likelihood, your first orgasm will come from your go-to moves—which again, for many women, will be clitoral stimulation. But what you do after that depends on what feels good in the moment.

If your clit feels like it’s pulsing with pleasure after your first go, experiment with continuing to coax a climax from that nerve-clustered cutie. If it feels good—or TBH, even hurts-so-good—keep on going, says Howard. Pro tip: “As you keep the stimulation going, breathe deeply, as your breath can help your body ride the next wave when it comes,” she says.

But if it is pulsing in pain, Suwinyattichaiporn recommends switching it up. “This could include, but is not limited to, going into an extended make-out session, narrowing in on breast and nipple play, or using mouths and fingers on the surrounding tissues,” she says.

During partnered play, you can also shift from clit-focused stimulation to penetrative sex, says Suwinyattichaiporn. The position is up to you, but “doggy style is an easy sex position to do while using a vibrator on the clitoris,” she says, if you want to reintroduce that type of stimulation as you try for round two.

Multiple O’s don’t just feel good, they can also be kinky AF—if you’re into that.

“Multiple orgasms can intersect with power dynamics in ways that are really pleasurable for those into BDSM,” says Howard. It all comes down to the process of who is giving, who is receiving, who is leading the pace, and the energy between you in the moment, she says.

In a pre-existing Dominant/submissive dynamic, for example, a Dominant might prolong the time between the first and subsequent orgasms, or withhold a second or third orgasm altogether to emphasize erotic control, says Howard. Meanwhile, a service-oriented submissive might be “forced” (within negotiated boundaries) to help their Dominant orgasm again and again, long after their hand cramps or tongue tires, as a way of demonstrating devotion.

Important note: Whether you’re utilizing multiple orgasms (or lack thereof) or whips, chains, and handcuffs, “healthy power dynamics are not about manipulation, but about mutual trust and consent, pleasure as communication, not punishment (unless that’s your thing!),” Howard says. So, any use of orgasm control or denial must be explicitly negotiated beforehand and include ongoing check-ins, safewords, and aftercare.

“Experiencing multi-orgasms is a process; almost no one achieves this on the first try,” says Richmond.

There will likely be a learning curve where you have some one-and-done, mediocre, and no-orgasm sessions. But every experience teaches your body something about what it likes, what it needs, what it responds to, and what it doesn’t, says Richmond.

Stay curious to avoid getting frustrated (sexual or otherwise!). “The more you can relax, open up to all pleasure possibilities, and enjoy the moment for what it is, whether it’s a single or multiple orgasmic experience, you set yourself up for more pleasure going forward,” she says. “Not every sexual experience is going to be a ten out of ten, but every sexual experience can be a good sexual experience if we allow it to be.”

There are a million different reasons multiple O’s may not be in the cards the first time you try. “Sex and orgasms are full-body experiences; the state of your mind, body, and hormones all play a role,” says Howard.

Case in point: “Dehydration can lower blood flow and make tissues less responsive,” she says. Even being a little parched can interfere with Mission Multiple Orgasms.

“Stress raises cortisol, which can block arousal altogether,” she adds, so a high-stress week may mean your body simply doesn’t have the capacity to go beyond one orgasm that day. Further, “where you are in your menstrual cycle often matters, too, since many people notice higher libido and easier orgasms around ovulation when estrogen peaks,” says Howard.

In other words, if multiple O’s don’t happen on the first, second, or tenth try, it doesn’t mean you did anything wrong, or that you’ll never climb two peaks in one sesh. It just means your body has been communicating where it was at, and what it needed, the times you’ve tried.

#5075. Bitcoin Short-Term Holders Send $3.6 Billion in BTC to Exchanges at a Loss

Bitcoin Short-Term Holders Send $3.6 Billion in BTC to Exchanges at a Loss

Bitcoin investors who bought recently are moving tens of thousands of coins to cryptocurrency exchanges at a loss, adding to concerns about selling pressure as the cryptocurrency falls toward the low-$70,000 range.

CryptoQuant’s latest short-term holder profit-and-loss chart shows roughly 47,300 BTC transferred to exchanges below their acquisition price over 24 hours, compared with only about 1,100 BTC arriving at a profit.

Combined inflows stood near 48,500 BTC, or around $3.6 billion at Bitcoin’s current price of approximately $74,000.

The research company's chart below shows transfers, not completed sales. Sending Bitcoin to an exchange can be the first step toward selling, but some deposits are made for trading for other tokens, collateral, or other services. The data also does not establish exactly how much of the transferred Bitcoin was subsequently sold.

The distinction is important because the chart measures the behavior of short-term holders, a group that CryptoQuant generally defines as investors holding Bitcoin for less than 155 days.

These are often the buyers most exposed to a sudden price reversal, particularly when their holdings are worth less than they paid.

In a November 11 CryptoQuant analysis, contributor Amr Taha identified two large waves of underwater exchange deposits over the previous week: 50,790 BTC on November 3 and 46,060 BTC on November 10.

“On November 10, profitable transfers stood near 1,700 BTC, meaning approximately 27 BTC arrived at a loss for every 1 BTC transferred at a profit,” Taha wrote.

That is an unusually one-sided flow. Investors moving coins into exchanges are overwhelmingly doing so at prices below those at which they acquired them, rather than cashing in gains from the earlier rally.

A similar pattern has occurred over several months, with recurring spikes in loss-making exchange transfers as Bitcoin’s price swung higher and lower. The latest red spike appears near the deepest readings visible since July.

A separate CryptoQuant chart breaks down deposits to Binance by the time since each Bitcoin last moved on-chain. It shows 3,290 BTC arriving from coins last moved one day to one week earlier on November 1, followed by a much larger 8,114 BTC from the same category on November 3.

On November 9, the platform also received 1,925 BTC from coins last moved one to three months earlier, according to Taha’s analysis. Those figures help identify which age groups are active, but they do not necessarily reveal when the owners originally purchased their Bitcoin.

The Binance chart measures one exchange and groups coins by their last on-chain movement. The profit-and-loss charts track short-term holders sending coins to exchanges more broadly.

The timing matters because Bitcoin has been sliding from its late-September highs above $85,000 into the $73,000-to-$74,000 area visible at the end of the screenshots. A newer buyer who paid $80,000 would be sitting on a paper loss of roughly 7.5% with Bitcoin at $74,000; selling at that price would lock it in.

The recent weakness is not limited to short-term holders. Glassnode identified about 4 million BTC held by longer-term investors that had been acquired between $85,000 and $118,000, potentially leaving another pocket of underwater supply above current prices.

Exodus targets $30 million in annual savings as StoneX keeps its $12 price target and sees adjusted breakeven on the horizon.

StoneX kept a Buy rating and a $12 price target for Exodus Movement Exodus Movement, Inc. (EXOD) on Thursday.

The basis of its argument is that Exodus' push into payments and lower costs are helping offset weaker activity in its legacy crypto wallet business, bringing it closer to breaking even on an adjusted basis. "The cost reset is real," StoneX analyst Mark Palmer wrote in a Thursday report.

Exodus shares were up over 8% to $4.68 on Thursday morning, with StoneX's $12 target implying a further gain of around 150%.

The company reported third-quarter earnings on Monday, reaching revenue of $32.5 million and topping analyst estimates of between $29 million and $31 million. Its adjusted EBITDA loss shrank to $3.9 million from $6.7 million in the second quarter, while its net loss totaled $8.3 million.

Exodus' payments segment contributed nearly $11 million in its first full quarter following the acquisitions of Monavate and Baanx in May. That helped offset a 29% year-over-year drop-off in wallet-related revenue to $21.6 million. The company noted that around 64% of payments revenue came from traditional payments, while the other 36% came from onchain finance.

Exodus also cut its headcount from 326 to 214 employees between May and Monday's earnings, with management expecting roughly $30 million in savings going into 2027.

"Our cost base is lower. If exchange volumes rise, revenue can grow without a proportionate increase in overhead," CEO JP Richardson said on Monday's earnings call.

However, Richardson also warned that many of the company's milestones still depend on regulatory approval and execution.

Exodus is still waiting on approval in Latvia that would allow the company to resume issuing its payment cards in Europe. The company said it has already resumed issuing cards in the U.S.

#5067. Stablecoin Drain, ETF Outflows Leave Bitcoin 'Running on Empty'

Stablecoin Drain, ETF Outflows Leave Bitcoin 'Running on Empty'

The pool of dollars that fuels crypto trading is draining.

Total stablecoin supply fell by $14.2 billion over the 30 days through Nov. 9, the fastest contraction since June 2022, according to a report published Wednesday by onchain analytics firm Glassnode.

The total stablecoin supply now sits just below $280 billion, and the research company suggests that's a big reason why Bitcoin is currently "running on empty."

Stablecoins, tokens typically pegged to the U.S. dollar, serve as crypto’s cash. Traders park money in them on exchanges and onchain before buying other assets.

When the stablecoin supply shrinks, it means holders are redeeming tokens for dollars and pulling that money out of crypto entirely, which Glassnode said leaves “less fuel for a rally.” The firm said a return to supply growth would be the first sign that fresh money is coming in.

The stablecoin drain isn’t the only sign of money leaving. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.6 billion in net redemptions over 20 trading sessions, the report found, and Bitcoin has traded at a discount on Coinbase, a gauge of U.S. demand.

Bitcoin also slipped below the average cost basis of short-term holders, about $75,600, leaving most recent buyers underwater, and traded near $73,000 on Wednesday. Over $3.6 billion in Bitcoin was recently moved over to exchanges, potentially to be sold, at a loss, according to a separate report from CryptoQuant today.

Glassnode did point to some silver linings. The slide has flushed out most leveraged long positions near the current price, buy orders outweigh sell orders in the order book, and wallets holding between 100 and 10,000 BTC have started accumulating.

The firm flagged $73,000 as key support and said the broader trend change that began in August remains intact as long as Bitcoin holds above its June low.

The contraction comes as stablecoins face growing competition from traditional finance.

Global banks including Goldman Sachs, Citi, and Deutsche Bank are backing a $270 million challenge to crypto’s dominant stablecoin issuers, and the Federal Reserve recently unveiled stablecoin rules on reserves and capital.

#5065. NYDFS sets fresh advertising standards for crypto firms

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The NYDFS issued the guidance, outlining its expectations for "accurate content, clear disclosures, and an established governance and review process."

New York’s top financial regulator issued fresh guidance outlining expectations for cryptocurrency firms to establish policies and procedures governing advertising and marketing, including measures to prevent false or misleading content, by next year.

On Thursday, the New York State Department of Financial Services issued the guidance, outlining its expectations for "accurate content, clear disclosures, and an established governance and review process," with firms expected to implement the measures by May 10, 2027.

"For more than a decade, DFS has set the global standard for virtual currency oversight and consumer protection," said Department of Financial Services Acting Superintendent Kaitlin Asrow in the statement. "Today’s guidance reinforces our commitment to protecting New Yorkers and ensures advertising and marketing practices in the virtual currency space are clear, comprehensive, and accessible."

NYDFS has long been a leading regulator of the crypto industry, launching its landmark BitLicense regime in 2015 and helping shape the broader regulatory approach to digital assets in the U.S. Other regulators globally have also turned their attention to advertisements, including in the UK.

The NY regulator said virtual currency entities' advertising should not be deceptive, should not include any exaggerated claims or say that something has no fee trading while omitting other costs. The entities also need to file disclosures on if people making testimonials were compensated, the regulator said.

Entities also need to have a governance and review structure that includes clear roles and responsibilities for advertising and marketing, the regulator added.

#5064. Meet the Athlete-Turned-Founder Rethinking the Sports Bra

Sara Falkson turned her own experience with ill-fitting sports bras into a brand focused on better support.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

When an athlete suits up for competition, every piece of equipment matters. A shin guard that doesn’t fit or football pads that aren’t secured properly can affect performance. But one essential piece of gear is often overlooked: the sports bra.

When shopping at top athletic retailers, female athletes are usually presented with around three options: small, medium, and large. The cup size grows with the band size, and there is little to no room for customization.

Anyone with breasts can relate to struggling to find a comfortable bra, but it's an unfortunate reality many female athletes have come to accept. What may seem like a trivial aspect of an athlete’s uniform holds a much greater significance. According to a 2021 survey of 70 elite athletes conducted by the English Institute of Sport, 75 percent of athletes have never been fitted for a sports bra, and 26 percent reported that breast pain affects their athletic performance. This speaks to the bigger picture that 46 percent of school-aged women said their breasts were a barrier to exercise.

Trying to find the solution for an ill-fitting sports bra often falls on the consumer—but Robyn founder Sara Falkson wants to change that.

Falkson grew up in a bustling home filled with love for sports and love for one another. In the middle with three older brothers and one younger sister, she tried almost every sport, but eventually settled on field hockey.

Playing sports while going through puberty is something all adolescents have to deal with, and Falkson was no exception. Navigating a changing body and mind can be tricky, especially when you’re relying on that body to remember plays and execute drills.

When it came time for the obligatory mall trip to shop for sports bras, Falkson says she found herself relying on her teammates.

“My teammates were really the best sisters of all,” she tells Women’s Health. “I found such a family and community in sports.”

Her teammates offered emotional support, but Falkson still struggled to find physical support.

“I have a bigger chest, and when it came to finding a sports bra, I struggled so deeply,” she says. “I would either find some that would be over-sexualizing, or I would chafe, and I would get horrible scars and stabs.”

A solution she and her mom came up with was for her to wear two sports bras while playing field hockey. Although this remedy did solve the support issue, Falkson says it came with another set of issues—teammates commented on it, and it wasn’t comfortable.

“Early on, I just assumed that my body’s probably the problem,” she says. “This gear design came from huge companies. They know what they’re doing, and I’m probably the one not fitting the mold.”

This prompted her to look toward other solutions, such as modifying her fueling regime to change her body, as well as the more permanent solution of a breast reduction.

These experiences stayed with Falkson, including through her time on the Dartmouth College field hockey team. She says that even in college, a lot of her teammates were still navigating the same issues of pressure and appearance on the field.

“We tried to do as much as possible to bring in the resources on the mental performance side and the nutrition side at Dartmouth and really switch the language [to] being more performance-focused, rather than appearance,” she says.

After earning her undergraduate degree, Falkson moved to San Francisco and began coaching youth field hockey on the side. She loved coaching, but soon heard familiar concerns from her players.

“The girls were saying the exact same things,” she says. “They were focused more on how they looked in their uniforms than how they were actually performing, and by that point, I’m like, ‘This is ridiculous. Why do so many of us feel this way?’”

Motivated by her frustration, Falkson enrolled at Harvard University to pursue a master’s degree in design and engineering. While there, a single stat stayed on her mind: 50 percent of girls drop out of sport by age 14 due to low body confidence, according to research conducted by Dove.

“It was so heartbreaking, but also in some ways validating knowing that there’s some research grounded in this, and we’re not alone,” she says.

While at Harvard, Falkson started leading body confidence workshops in the Boston area where she sat down with young female athletes to get a better understanding on what could be done to help them feel better about playing sports.

“We put the ownership in their hands, and that’s how we got Robyn,” she says of her company, an athletic brand dedicated to designing sports bras that elevate body confidence on and off the field. “Time after time, girls were so frustrated about sports bras, because they’re an essential piece of equipment, but so overlooked.”

Robyn started as a master’s thesis proposal. Falkson’s advisor, Karen Korellis Reuther, played a key role.

Korellis Reuther is extremely familiar with the world of design. Now a Design Critic in Architecture in Harvard’s Graduate School of Design, she is a designer by training and practice for over 40 years and is a former creative executive at Nike and Reebok.

Korellis Reuther’s passion for helping to redesign the world for women also was fueled by personal experience.

“As designers, we really connect very much to the built world, and that’s where Sara really had a passion,” Korellis Reuther says, “We were trying to understand what around the built world—whether it’s what we put on our bodies, what we use, or what we surround ourselves with—that is causing girls’ body confidence to drop, and what could we do from a design perspective?”

Korellis Reuther’s research in women-focused design goes far beyond sports. She is the author of Man-Made: How We Designed a World That Leaves Women Out, and How We Can Make It Right.

In the book, Korellis Reuther explores how women are still grossly unaccounted for in the world of design, despite inhabiting the same world as men. Her research examines how components of everyday life inconvenience women—and sometimes endanger them.

From poor locker room setups to unfit fire equipment gear for female firefighters and personal protection equipment, women are consistently thought of as an afterthought when it comes to design.

“We think, ‘I can’t make this work,’” she says. “But the truth is, it wasn’t designed for us in the first place.”

While working as a design executive for major brands, Korellis Reuther had a unique perspective being physically present where these decisions were made, but still not feeling like change was taking place. She speaks openly about feeling dismissed and looked over in important meetings, despite her high-ranking position in the company.

“The story had to be told, and as a designer, I knew we could make it right.”

Robyn is set to release a high-support bra, the Game Day, and a medium-support bra, the Everyday. The principal component that makes Robyn bras different from other athletic brands is the adjustability features. Almost all components of the bra will be adjustable, including the shoulder straps and bottom band.

In preliminary testing, subjects ran at 8 MPH pace while hooked up to machines to monitor their breast movement. The Robyn Bra demonstrated approximately 46 percent less vertical breast movement than a leading high-support sports bra.

Robyn encompasses Falkson, including the brand’s name. She and her mother share Robyn as a middle name. It proved to have a double meaning after Korellis Reuther pointed out that the robin is one of the only species of bird with a distinct breast that is a different color then the rest of the body.

“Just like the robin bird, we want our athlete to lead with color, joy, and strength,” Falkson says.

Robyn has certainly been led with strength thus far. With the product set to launch this spring, Robyn has already amassed over 34,000 followers on Instagram as well as more than 100 brand ambassadors comprised of high school and college athletes.

It has already been a part of five major collaborations, and was featured in Yankees Mag—another element that’s personal to Falkson, as her longtime boyfriend, Ben Rice, plays professional baseball for the New York Yankees.

The couple went to high school together and began dating early in college. Rice will make the occasional surprise appearance on Robyn’s social media, like when Falkson handmade a coffee table to celebrate his anniversary with the team. She also filmed a series in which she 3D printed custom Yankees memorabilia for this year’s playoff season.

Both athletes, Falkson says the two are able to push each other to be better, and seeing Rice’s career expand has made her more inspired about her own.

“I think if anything, seeing his career has just inspired me to also push to see my dreams and take risks,” she says.

Robyn isn’t just about apparel—it has its own mission, too. The company has hosted dozens of body confidence workshops, including a recent partnership with South Bronx United, a soccer nonprofit.. Robyn teamed up with South Bronx United as well as mental health organization The Hidden Opponent to create a four week-curriculum focused on empowering young athletes.

Robyn also works with Shooting Touch, a basketball nonprofit out of Boston for body confidence-focused programming. Falkson also coordinated a partnership with a 3D printing company to bring the printers into local schools to encourage more girls to get into STEM.

“For me personally, I’m glad our world has Saras, because we need to keep pushing forward,” Korellis Reuther says. “She pushes forward from her true, authentic self—with a sense of joy, with a sense of optimism, but also a sense of, ‘We haven’t done it right yet, and we need to make this right.’”

Looking toward the future, Falkson says her dream scenario would be to have Robyn gear become part of a female athlete’s uniform. “Once I see Robyn in the real world and girls connecting and bonding over a body confident sport and feeling their best, celebrating lifelong movement, that’s my goal.”

Through it all, Falkson has held on to the perspective she had as a young athlete.

“Growing up and being a woman is hard,” she says. “What you’re going through is okay and normal, and you’re still going to be valued. Giving girls the resources to have that space is really important.”

Alyssa Cruz is the social media editor at Women’s Health, where she drives social strategy and helps shape the brand’s voice across platforms. Prior to WH, she worked at CBS News and palabra. Originally from Toledo, she is a proud alumna of Ohio University, where she received two bachelor’s degrees in journalism and Spanish. When she is not creating social content, you can find her trying different restaurants in New York City or planning her next trip.

#5063. Your ‘Healthy’ Soda Alternative May Not Be So Healthy for Your Liver

WE'RE LONG PAST having to make a decision between just Coke or Pepsi. You've got a lot of options if you're bored of water—from fizzy nonalcoholic drinks to flavored seltzers. But while a lot of these drinks market themselves as healthy alternatives to sodas, research suggests they could be silently doing a number on your liver health.

If you've done the work to switch to diet sodas or have learned to love prebiotic sodas like Olipop or Poppi, the news can be pretty hard to hear. So it's important to get this out of the way upfront: If you have a "healthy" soda every once in a while, the experts we spoke to say the risk of having liver problems is unlikely. The real danger to your liver is if you’re tossing these back on a daily or near-daily basis. Here's everything you need to know.

THE STUDY PRESENTED at the United European Gastroenterology (UEG) Week 2025 used data from 103,251 people who enrolled in the UK Biobank and didn’t have liver disease at the start of the study. Participants were asked to fill out questionnaires about what they ate and drank throughout the day. The researchers then paid close attention to how much sugar-sweetened and low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages they drank.

The major takeaway from the study is that it pretty much linked any drink that wasn’t water to metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD). You might know it by its former name, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD). But whatever you call it, it's no joke.

MASLD happens when too much fat builds up in your liver from inputs other than alcohol. It can lead to serious liver scarring or end-stage liver disease. It can also put you at high risk for heart disease and diabetes. According to the American College of Gastroenterology, heart disease is the number one killer of people living with MASLD.

DAILY DRINKING OF nearly 9 ounces of any type of drink that was not water was associated with a significantly higher risk of developing MASLD. That risk jumped 50 percent for sugar-sweetened beverages and was surprisingly higher—60 percent—for those who drank low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages. (Though some of these prebiotic sodas have more sugar and less fiber than marketing claims might imply).

After a 10-year follow-up, 949 people developed MASLD and 103 died from liver-related causes. Both types of beverages were significantly connected to increases in liver fat.

Replacing nearly 12 ounces of sugary beverages with water lowered MASLD risk by 14.4 percent and by 13.5 percent with low- or non-sugar-sweetened drinks. But if you switched completely from a sugary beverage like soda to a low-sugar beverage like fruit juice, the authors found that this did not help lower a person's risk of developing MASLD.

Exactly which beverages raised risk is hard to say because the authors did not directly state what low- or no-sugar drinks people were drinking. That said, there are lots of sodas that are marketed as being healthier alternatives that are not as good as they claim, according to Danbee Kim, MD, nutrition expert and assistant professor at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School.

“Even beverages marketed as ‘natural’ or ‘health-oriented’ may still fall into this category if they rely on sweeteners instead of sugar,” Kim says. “The key commonality is that they provide sweetness without significant calories. However, they may still affect liver health.”

A FEW THINGS may be going on here. “Low/non-sugar sweetened beverages, although low in calories, may still affect liver health through several pathways,” says Lihe Liu, a graduate student researcher at The First Affiliated Hospital of Soochow University in China and lead study author. “They can alter the gut microbiome, interfere with feelings of fullness, increase preference for sweet foods, and in some cases stimulate insulin secretion.”

When you have something that’s sweet, even if it doesn’t contain much or any sugar, it sets off a chain reaction in your body the same way as if you were to actually have sugar, says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, registered dietitian and co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab. “You’re basically tricking your body,” he says. “Your body still reacts as if it has sugar.”

As a result, Matheny says your body produces more insulin (a hormone that helps regulate blood sugar), and encourages the liver to take in more sugar from your bloodstream. This can lead to metabolic disruption.

“Some artificial sweeteners may alter how the body handles glucose and insulin, contributing to insulin resistance, which is a key driver of metabolic dysfunction and fat accumulation in the liver,” says Kim. This process may even mess with your appetite, making you think that you're hungry when you're not.

Another reason has nothing to do with your gut, but with your mind. Kim says we can't discount the possibility that people who drink these low- and non-sugar-sweetened beverages may subconsciously compensate for the “saved” calories by eating more sugary or fatty foods later.

Keep in mind that all this is speculation. The explanation for why and how artificially sweetened beverages are linked to MASLD is still under investigation since Liu says her team didn't look too deep into this.

NOT NECESSARILY. “THEY’RE definitely an improvement over traditional diet sodas,” Matheny says. “That’s not to say that they’re a totally healthy option.”

When given a choice, water reigns supreme. And even then you still don't want to go too overboard.

If you still want to crack open a cranberry fizz prebiotic soda or even an unsweetened iced tea, Matheny says that’s probably fine, too. “In moderation, kind of everything is OK.”

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#5061. Avalanche Owns Five Percent of Tokenized Stocks

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of Tokenization Briefing

Over this past week, three U.S. tokenized stock venues picked three different blockchains to launch on. The WSJ reported Monday that NYSE chose Avalanche as the sole settlement chain for its planned 24/7 platform. On November 4, $CNTN became the first U.S. equity to trade under the SEC's Innovation Exemption. Five North sold tokenized $CNTN shares to DRW Cumberland on TSV, a permissioned venue on the Canton Network. On Monday, Formation notified the SEC that it will run a venue on Solana, targeting early 2027.

Given the strict regulations surrounding tokenized stocks in the U.S., no venue is going multichain at launch. Besides increasing complexity for regulatory compliance, fracturing activity across different networks would make it difficult to build deep liquidity while staying under the SEC's strict 0.25% average daily volume cap per asset.

The leading chains by tokenized stock volume have benefited from large exchanges launching their own tokenized stock products and leveraging their existing user bases.

The chains that successfully gain market share will be the ones that attract the biggest platforms and issuers.

Tokenized stocks have matched the growth of stablecoins closely. 240 days after crossing $1 billion, stablecoins stood at roughly $4 billion and tokenized stocks stood at $3.7 billion, while tokenized Treasuries were at $2.5 billion at the same point and have since fallen well behind. Although tokenized stocks may not continue at this growth rate, the trend is promising.

The count of tokenized stocks and ETFs worth more than $1 million climbed from three in Jan 2025 to 287 as of Nov 8, across roughly 4,400 tokenized assets and a $3.7 billion market.

Distribution keeps broadening too. HTX launched spot trading in bStocks on Monday with 20 names including NVDA, TSLA, QQQ, and CRCL. Strategy and SpaceX are now the most tokenized issuers by asset count. Our take is that breadth is the healthier signal for a young market, because a long tail of products with real size offers a better user experience than one or two large wrappers.
